 (a-c)s\ne\Lambda(t)\bigr],\qquad
 \forall s,t\ \exists\mathbf u\ \bigl[\mathcal E(c;\mathbf u)\wedge
 (a-c)s\ne\Lambda(t)\bigr],
\]
where $\mathbf u$ is a quintuple of witnesses. In the first form the witnesses
for $\Xi(c)$ do not occur in the universal inequality; in the second, a
single pair $(s,t)$ already produces witnesses for $\Xi(c)$, and conversely a
fixed witness works for all $(s,t)$. Both are correct by
\cref{odg:def:thm:constants,odg:def:cor:universal}. Source 07 claimed neither
minimality nor the impossibility of an existential definition; source 06's
\cref{odg:def:thm:ctgraph} supplies one.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Homomorphisms preserve the constant term}

\begin{theorem}[All homomorphisms respect the constant term \src{06}]\label{odg:def:thm:homct}
Let $\Bbbk,\Bbbk'$ be fields of characteristic zero containing $\sqrt2$, and
$\Gamma,\Gamma'$ ordered abelian groups. Every unital ring homomorphism
$f:\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)\to\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk',\Gamma')$ satisfies
$\ct(f(x))=\ct(x)$. If $i\in\Bbbk\cap\Bbbk'$ and
$f:\mathcal R_{\Z[i]}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)\to\mathcal R_{\Z[i]}(\Bbbk',\Gamma')$ is a
unital ring homomorphism, then $\ct(f(x))=\sigma(\ct(x))$, where $\sigma$ is
the identity or complex conjugation on $\Z[i]$, according as $f(i)=i$ or
$f(i)=-i$. No size restriction on the target is imposed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $x=n+a$ with $n\in\mathfrak o$ and $a$ in the ideal. By
\cref{odg:def:cor:idealhom}, $f(a)$ lies in the target ideal. A unital
homomorphism fixes ordinary integers. In the Gaussian case $f(i)^2=-1$, and by
\cref{odg:def:lem:units} a root of $T^2+1$ in the target ring is a constant,
hence $\pm i$. Taking constant terms of $f(x)=f(n)+f(a)$ gives the claim.
\end{proof}

Source 06 states the theorem for the rings of its arithmetic sections; the
proof uses only that both coefficient fields contain $\sqrt2$. It generalizes
\cref{odg:prop:canonicalct} (endomorphisms of $\Oz$) and source 07's statement
that ring automorphisms of $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ preserve
$\mathfrak o$, $I$ and $\ct$ up to $\sigma$, which source 07 derives from the
parameter-free definitions. It does not say that homomorphisms kill the ideal:
the identity does not. Preservation of the ideal, annihilation of the ideal and
pointwise fixation of the real coefficients are three different assertions
\src{06}. For set-sized $\Gamma'$ it is consistent with
\cref{odg:fact:settarget}: every unital homomorphism
$\Oz\to\mathcal R_\Z(\R,\Gamma')$ is then $x\mapsto\ct(x)1$. Source 07's
version concerns ring automorphisms of the coefficient-restricted ring; it
does not classify field automorphisms of $\No$ or $\No[i]$, nor claim that an
arbitrary field automorphism preserves $\Oz$ or $\Og$.

\subsection{Number-field coefficient rings}

The polynomial $\Lambda$ cannot serve every number field, since a field
containing $\sqrt{13}$ has a root of it. A tailored polynomial of the same
shape exists for each fixed number field \src{07}.

\begin{lemma}[A tailored intersective polynomial \src{07}]\label{odg:def:lem:tailored}
For every number field $K$ there are distinct primes $p,q$ such that
$\Lambda_K(T)=(T^2-p)(T^2-q)(T^2-pq)$ has no root in $K$ and has a root modulo
every positive integer.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A number field has only finitely many quadratic subfields: it lies in a finite
Galois extension of $\Q$, whose Galois group has finitely many subgroups. By
Dirichlet's theorem choose a prime $p\equiv1\pmod8$ with
$\Q(\sqrt p)\not\subseteq K$, and then a prime $q\equiv1\pmod{8p}$ with
$\Q(\sqrt q),\Q(\sqrt{pq})\not\subseteq K$; only finitely many $q$ are
excluded, since distinct values give distinct squarefree radicands $q$ and
$pq$. Then none of $p,q,pq$ is a square in $K$. For an odd prime
$\ell\notin\{p,q\}$, one of $p,q,pq$ is a nonzero square modulo $\ell$, as in
\cref{odg:def:prop:intersective}. At $\ell=p$ use $q\equiv1\pmod p$. At
$\ell=q$, quadratic reciprocity and $p\equiv1\pmod4$ give
$(p/q)=(q/p)=(1/p)=1$. The nonzero roots lift to all odd prime powers, and at
$2$ the number $p\equiv1\pmod8$ has a root modulo every $2^a$ by the lifting
step of \cref{odg:def:prop:intersective}. The Chinese remainder theorem
finishes the proof. Dirichlet's theorem and reciprocity are classical inputs
\cite{Apostol}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Coefficient rings in number fields \src{07}]\label{odg:def:thm:numberfield}
Let $\mathfrak o$ be a unital subring of a number field $K$, let $\Bbbk\supseteq
K$ be a field of characteristic zero, and let $A$ be an intermediate ring for
$\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$. Let $\Xi_K$ be $\Xi$ with $\Lambda$ replaced by
$\Lambda_K$. Then $\Xi_K$ defines $\mathfrak o$ in $A$. If moreover
$A=\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ and $\Bbbk$ contains a root of
$\Lambda_K$, then $\ct(a)\ne0\Leftrightarrow\exists s,t\ (as=\Lambda_K(t))$,
and the ideal test, the universal definition of the ideal and both prenex
definitions of the graph of $\ct$ hold with $\Lambda_K$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The proof of \cref{odg:def:thm:constants} used Pell rigidity
(\cref{odg:def:cor:pell}), the absence of roots of the polynomial in
$\mathfrak o$ (\cref{odg:def:lem:tailored,odg:def:lem:units}), a nonzero integer
in every $x\mathfrak o$ (\cref{odg:def:lem:intmultiple}) and roots modulo all
integers; it applies verbatim. For the detector, given $c\ne0$ in
$\mathfrak o$ choose a positive integer $m\in c\mathfrak o$ and $b\in\Z$ with
$\Lambda_K(b)\in m\Z$, and apply \cref{odg:def:thm:augdetector} with any root of
$\Lambda_K$ in $\Bbbk$. The ideal test and the graph use only the detector and
the constant definition.
\end{proof}

The theorem covers subrings of a number field that are not finitely
generated. It does not give one polynomial uniform in all number fields, and
does not apply as stated to algebraic extensions of $\Q$ of infinite degree:
no polynomial of this kind is root-free in a field containing all algebraic
numbers \src{07}.

\begin{remark}[Existential definitions over number-field coefficients \mergetag]\label{odg:def:rem:numberfieldideal}
The formula $\exists y\,(x^2=2y^2)$ of \cref{odg:def:thm:ideal} fails when
$\sqrt2\in\Frac(\mathfrak o)$, for instance for $\mathfrak o=\Z[\sqrt2]$.
In the setting of \cref{odg:def:thm:numberfield} choose a prime $\delta$ with
$\Q(\sqrt\delta)\not\subseteq K$; it exists because $K$ has finitely many
quadratic subfields and distinct primes give distinct fields
$\Q(\sqrt\delta)$. Then $\delta$ is not a square in $K\supseteq\Frac(\mathfrak o)$,
since $z^2=\delta$ with $z\in K$ would give $\Q(\sqrt\delta)=\Q(z)\subseteq K$.
If $\sqrt\delta\in\Bbbk$, \cref{odg:def:rem:delta} shows that
$\exists y\,(x^2=\delta y^2)$ defines $\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$ in
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$, and
$\Xi_K(n)\wedge\exists y\,((x-n)^2=\delta y^2)$ defines the graph of $\ct$:
the first clause says $n\in\mathfrak o$, by \cref{odg:def:thm:numberfield}
applied to the intermediate ring $\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$, and
the second says $x-n\in\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$. Both definitions are Diophantine;
they improve source 07's universal and prenex formulas whenever
$\sqrt\delta\in\Bbbk$, and need no root of $\Lambda_K$ in $\Bbbk$.
\end{remark}

\section{Coefficient reconstruction and logical consequences}\label{odg:def:sec:reconstruction}

\subsection{The multiplier ring of the ideal}

Throughout this subsection $\Gamma\ne0$, $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ is a
unital subring, $\mathcal R=\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$,
$I=\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$, $\mathcal A=\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$, and
$\mathcal F=\Frac(\mathcal R)$, viewed inside the Hahn field of all series with
reverse-well-ordered support in $\Gamma$ \src{06}. Put
$(I:I)_{\mathcal F}=\{r\in\mathcal F:rI\subseteq I\}$.

\begin{lemma}[\src{06}]\label{odg:def:lem:ainfrac}
$\mathcal A\subseteq\mathcal F$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose $g\in\Gamma^{>0}$. For $a\in\mathcal A$ both $\omega^g$ and $a\omega^g$
lie in $I\subseteq\mathcal R$, so $a=(a\omega^g)/\omega^g\in\mathcal F$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The multiplier identity \src{06}]\label{odg:def:thm:multiplier}
Inside $\mathcal F$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:def:eq:multiplier}
 (I:I)_{\mathcal F}=\mathcal A,\qquad
 \Bbbk=\{0\}\cup\bigl((I:I)_{\mathcal F}\bigr)^\times .
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A nonnegative and a strictly positive exponent add to a strictly positive
exponent, so $\mathcal AI\subseteq I$. Conversely let
$r\in\mathcal F\setminus\mathcal A$ and choose a negative exponent $h$ in
$\supp(r)$; it need not be the least, and there need not be a least. Then
$\omega^{-h}\in I$, and the coefficient of $r\omega^{-h}$ at exponent zero is
the nonzero coefficient of $\omega^h$ in $r$, so $r\omega^{-h}\notin I$. The
second identity follows from $\mathcal A^\times=\Bbbk^\times$
(\cref{odg:def:lem:units}).
\end{proof}

An integral domain interprets its fraction field by pairs $(a,b)$ with
$b\ne0$, modulo $ad=bc$, with the usual operations; for the proper classes
``quotient'' means this definable presentation, with set-valued
representatives supplied by the surreal quotient, and no proper class is used
as an element of another \src{06}. With $\mathsf{Inf}$ from
\cref{odg:def:thm:ideal}, put
\begin{align*}
 \mathsf{Mult}(a,b)&:\Longleftrightarrow b\ne0\wedge\forall x\bigl(\mathsf{Inf}(x)
 \Rightarrow\exists y\,(\mathsf{Inf}(y)\wedge ax=by)\bigr),\\
 \mathsf{Coeff}(a,b)&:\Longleftrightarrow b\ne0\wedge\bigl(a=0\vee(a\ne0\wedge
 \mathsf{Mult}(a,b)\wedge\mathsf{Mult}(b,a))\bigr).
\end{align*}
The first says that the fraction $a/b$ multiplies $I$ into $I$, and is
invariant under change of representative; the second, by
\cref{odg:def:thm:multiplier}, says that $a/b\in\Bbbk$.

\begin{corollary}[Internal reconstruction \src{06}]\label{odg:def:cor:internal}
Under the hypotheses of \cref{odg:def:thm:ideal} (for instance in the real and
Gaussian rings), $\mathcal A$ and the actual embedded coefficient field
$\Bbbk$ are parameter-free first-order definable in the interpreted fraction
field, and the coefficient map $\mathcal A\to\Bbbk$ has the definable graph
$\{(a,c):a\in\mathcal A,\ c\in\Bbbk,\ a-c\in I\}$.
\end{corollary}

The formulas do not mention monomials; monomials are used only to prove that
they define the right sets, and $\sqrt2$ is not a hidden parameter. The
multiplier formula contains a universal quantifier, so the reconstruction is
first order and is not claimed positive existential \src{06}. Two boundaries
are necessary. If $\Gamma=0$, then $I=0$ and every fraction multiplies it, so
the formula recovers $\Frac(\mathfrak o)$ rather than $\Bbbk$. And $\mathcal F$
need not be the whole Hahn field: for $\Gamma=\Z$ it is the rational function
field $\Bbbk(\omega)$; the multiplier identity holds in $\mathcal F$ without any
such equality \src{06}. For example, in $\Z+\omega\R[\omega]$ the ideal
$\omega\R[\omega]$ is defined by $\mathsf{Inf}$, the fraction field is
$\R(\omega)$, the multiplier ring is $\R[\omega]$ and its units are $\R^\times$;
the reconstruction phenomenon is not an artifact of proper-class size. With
$\Gamma=\R$ and $f=\sum_{n\ge1}\omega^{1/n}$, the ideal formula is witnessed by
$f/\sqrt2$, and the multiplier proof uses one negative exponent, not a least
one \src{06}.

\subsection{Reconstructing the surreal field}

\Cref{odg:thm:fractions} gives $\Frac(\Oz)=\No$, with a common monomial
denominator for every set of surreals; applied to the set of real and
imaginary parts it gives $\Frac(\Og)=\No[i]$. Source 06 proves the same
statement (its common-denominator lemma), printed once as
\cref{odg:thm:fractions}; it uses bounds for sets of exponents only, and asserts
no common denominator for a proper class of inputs.

\begin{theorem}[Reconstruction of the real structure \src{06}]\label{odg:def:thm:realrecovery}
From the pure ring $\Oz$ one can parameter-free interpret the field $\No$ and
define in it the canonical subfield $\R$, the order, the ring
$\Ofin$ of finite elements, the ideal $\mathfrak m$ of infinitesimals and the
graph of the standard part $\st:\Ofin\to\R$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Interpret the fraction field, which is $\No$. By \cref{odg:def:cor:internal}
the predicate $\mathsf{Coeff}$ defines its subfield $\R$. Since $\No$ is real
closed \cite{Conway,Gonshor}, $x>0\Leftrightarrow x\ne0\wedge\exists z\,(z^2=x)$
defines the order. Put
$\Ofin(x):\Leftrightarrow\exists r\in\R\,(r>0\wedge-r\le x\le r)$ and
$\mathfrak m(x):\Leftrightarrow\forall r\in\R\,(r>0\Rightarrow-r<x<r)$. A
finite surreal has no positive exponents, and its coefficient at exponent zero
is a real number differing from it by an infinitesimal, unique since a nonzero
real is not infinitesimal. So
$\mathsf{ST}(x,r):\Leftrightarrow\Ofin(x)\wedge r\in\R\wedge\mathfrak m(x-r)$
defines the standard part.
\end{proof}

The two constant-term operations must not be confused
(\cref{odg:rem:notvaluation}): $\ct$ reads the constant term of a number with
nonnegative exponents, $\st$ that of a finite number, with nonpositive
exponents. For example $\ct(\omega+7)=7$ and $\st(7+\omega^{-1})=7$, but
$\omega+7$ is not finite \src{06}.

\begin{corollary}[The value group \src{06}]\label{odg:def:cor:valuegroup}
The natural ordered value group of $\No$ is interpretable from $\Oz$ as
$\No^\times/\Ofin^\times$, ordered by $v(x)\ge v(y)\Leftrightarrow x/y\in\Ofin$.
\end{corollary}

This recovers the value group as an interpreted quotient, not a canonical
cross-section of monomials, and does not show that the $\omega$-map, the
simplicity order, birthdays or the surreal exponential are definable
\src{06}. The fraction-field construction can be phrased as a canonical
bi-interpretation between the pure ring $\Oz$ and the field $\No$ with a
predicate for $\Oz$; the essential content is the reconstruction of the
\emph{canonical} coefficient field.

\subsection{Automorphisms of \texorpdfstring{$\Oz$}{Oz}}

\begin{theorem}[Automorphisms fix the real coefficients \src{06}]\label{odg:def:thm:autreal}
Every unital ring automorphism $f$ of $\Oz$ extends uniquely to a field
automorphism $\tilde f(a/b)=f(a)/f(b)$ of $\No$. It preserves the order, $\R$,
$\Ofin$ and $\mathfrak m$, fixes every real number, and commutes with $\st$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The extension is well defined by cross multiplication and unique because
every element of $\No$ is a fraction. It preserves the parameter-free
definable sets of \cref{odg:def:thm:realrecovery}, so it restricts to an
order-preserving automorphism of $\R$ fixing $\Q$. If $\tilde f(r)\ne r$, a
rational strictly between $r$ and $\tilde f(r)$ contradicts order
preservation. The definable graph of $\st$, with its real coordinate fixed,
gives $\st\circ\tilde f=\st$ on $\Ofin$.
\end{proof}

The statement concerns automorphisms. A universal multiplier formula need not
be preserved by a nonsurjective embedding, so the argument does not apply
unchanged to endomorphisms; \cref{odg:def:thm:homct} applies to all unital
homomorphisms \src{06}.

\begin{example}[Coefficient rigidity is not total rigidity \src{06}]\label{odg:def:ex:dilation}
The map $\mathsf S_2(\sum_ga_g\omega^g)=\sum_ga_g\omega^{2g}$ is a field
automorphism of $\No$ with inverse $\mathsf S_{1/2}$, since doubling exponents
is an order automorphism of $(\No,+)$; it preserves supports and $\Oz$, fixes
real coefficients and sends $\omega$ to $\omega^2$. So $\Aut(\Oz)$ is not
trivial.
\end{example}

The dilations $\mathsf S_a$ are studied in the repository's surcomplex
automorphism report (\fn{docs/surcomplex/surcomplex-field-automorphisms}), and
the single-dilation report (\fn{docs/surcomplex/single-dilation-hahn-support})
defines the coefficient field and the constant coefficient from one dilation of
a field; \cref{odg:def:thm:realrecovery} starts instead from the pure ring $\Oz$
and its Diophantine ideal. \Cref{odg:def:thm:autreal,odg:def:ex:dilation} are
also relevant to the questions on automorphisms of $\Oz$ studied in the
sibling report \fn{docs/surreal/set-sized-quotients-of-omnific-integers}.

\subsection{The Gaussian ring does not determine the real axis}

The Gaussian system and the multiplier theorem recover $\No[i]=\Frac(\Og)$ and
its actual coefficient field $\C$, but not the real form $\No$ \src{06}.

\begin{proposition}[All coefficient automorphisms occur \src{06}]\label{odg:def:prop:coeffaut}
Every field automorphism $\sigma$ of $\C$ extends coefficientwise to a field
automorphism $\hat\sigma(\sum_gc_g\omega^g)=\sum_g\sigma(c_g)\omega^g$ of
$\No[i]$ preserving $\Og$. Every automorphism of $\Og$ extends to $\No[i]$ and
preserves $\C$ setwise, so restriction to $\C$ is surjective and
$\sigma\mapsto\hat\sigma$ is a composition-preserving section.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A coefficient automorphism preserves supports and the finite sums and products
defining Hahn convolution; its inverse is $\widehat{\sigma^{-1}}$. It fixes $\Z$
and sends $i$ to $\pm i$, so it preserves $\Z[i]$ and hence $\Og$. An
automorphism of $\Og$ extends to the fraction field and preserves the
first-order coefficient predicate of \cref{odg:def:cor:internal}.
\end{proof}

For proper-class fields this is a statement about individual maps and their
compositions, not a construction of a set of all class automorphisms.

Let $\eta:\No\to\R$ send a surreal to the coefficient of $\omega^0$ in its
normal form. It is additive on $\No$ but not multiplicative
($\eta(\omega)=\eta(\omega^{-1})=0$, $\eta(1)=1$), and is used only as an
additive map on the exponent group. Put
$\chi(g)=\exp(i\pi\eta(g)/2)\in\C^\times$, the ordinary complex exponential of
an ordinary real multiple of $i$; then $\chi(g+h)=\chi(g)\chi(h)$, $\chi(0)=1$
and $\chi(1)=i$.

\begin{lemma}[A phase twist preserving $\Og$ \src{06}]\label{odg:def:lem:twist}
The map $\tau(\sum_gc_g\omega^g)=\sum_gc_g\chi(g)\omega^g$ is an automorphism of
$\No[i]$ that fixes $\C$ pointwise, preserves every support and $\Og$, and
satisfies $\tau(\omega)=i\omega$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
All values of $\chi$ are nonzero, so supports are unchanged; additivity is
clear. If $a+b=g$, the contribution of $(a,b)$ to the coefficient at $g$ is
multiplied by $\chi(a)\chi(b)=\chi(g)$, so $\tau$ commutes with convolution;
$\chi^{-1}$ gives the inverse. Constants sit at exponent zero and are fixed. A
Gaussian omnific integer keeps its positive support and its Gaussian integer
constant term.
\end{proof}

The character-twist construction and its use to move the real axis while
fixing complex coefficients are already in the repository's surcomplex
automorphism report (\fn{docs/surcomplex/surcomplex-field-automorphisms}); source
06 credits it there. The additional fact needed here is that the twist
preserves $\Og$.

\begin{theorem}[No definable real axis or conjugation \src{06}]\label{odg:def:thm:norealaxis}
The subfield $\No\subseteq\No[i]$ is not first-order definable in the structure
$(\No[i],\Og)$, even with every complex number named by a constant, and
neither is conjugation. The subring $\Oz\subseteq\Og$ is not definable in the
pure ring $\Og$, even with parameters from $\Z[i]$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The automorphism $\tau$ preserves the field, the predicate $\Og$ and every
complex constant, but sends $\omega\in\No$ to $i\omega\notin\No$; definable
sets are invariant under automorphisms fixing the parameters. Conjugation
does not commute with $\tau$: $\tau(\overline\omega)=i\omega$ while
$\overline{\tau(\omega)}=-i\omega$. Finally the restriction of $\tau$ to $\Og$
fixes $\Z[i]$ and moves $\omega\in\Oz$ outside $\Oz$.
\end{proof}

There is no conflict with the recovery of $\C$: the twist fixes $\C$
pointwise. The coefficient field and a distinguished real form are different
structure. Naming a suitable involution would suffice, since its fixed ring
can be taken, but that gives the real structure explicitly instead of
reconstructing it \src{06}.

\subsection{Logical consequences}

The equational transfer of \cref{odg:thm:transfer} is proved again by source 06
(its retraction-transfer lemma), by source 07 (positive-existential
conservation) and by source 13 (equation-only specialization and the
Hilbert's-tenth-problem transfer), for all rings
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ with parameters in $\mathfrak o$ and,
in the Gaussian case, with $\Z[i]$ in place of $\Z$. It is printed once, as
\cref{odg:thm:transfer,odg:cor:H10}; the proof is the same for every
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$. Transfer does not make solutions
constant: $y=x$ has many nonconstant solutions \src{07}.

\begin{theorem}[Diophantine subsets of $\Z^n$ \src{06}]\label{odg:def:thm:ce}
Let $\Bbbk$ be an ordered field containing $\sqrt2$. A subset $S\subseteq\Z^n$,
regarded as a subset of $\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)^n$ (in particular of
$\Oz^n$), is Diophantine there with integer coefficients if and only if it is
computably enumerable.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $S$ is defined by equations in $\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$, its trace on
$\Z^n$, which is $S$, is defined by the same equations over $\Z$, by
transfer; enumerating integer witnesses shows that it is computably
enumerable. Conversely, by the MRDP theorem \cite{Matiyasevich} (in its
integer form, by sign coding and sums of squares) $S$ is defined over $\Z$ by
an integer system $\Phi_S$. In $\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ impose
$\Std(x_1)\wedge\cdots\wedge\Std(x_n)\wedge\Phi_S(\mathbf x)$, with $\Std$ the
quintic \eqref{odg:def:eq:quintic}; once the free tuple is standard, transfer
makes the remaining condition equivalent to $\Phi_S$ over $\Z$. In the real
ring the conjunction can be written as one sum of squares.
\end{proof}

Here $S$ is required to consist of standard tuples. No computability
classification of arbitrary subsets of the ring is claimed, and arbitrary
surreal parameters are not finite inputs to an algorithm \src{06}.

\begin{corollary}[Standard arithmetic is definable \src{06, 07}]\label{odg:def:cor:arithmetic}
The structures $(\Z,+,\cdot,0,1)$ and $(\N,+,\cdot,0,1)$ are parameter-free
definable in the pure ring $\Oz$, and every arithmetic sentence has an
effective translation obtained by relativizing its quantifiers to $\Std$ and
to \cref{odg:def:cor:naturals}. For a set-sized intermediate ring $A$ for
$\Z\subseteq\R$, true arithmetic therefore reduces many-one to $\Th(A)$; so
$\Th(A)$ is undecidable and, being complete, is not recursively axiomatizable.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The translation replaces $\exists x$ by $\exists x(\Std(x)\wedge\cdots)$ and
$\forall x$ by $\forall x(\Std(x)\Rightarrow\cdots)$; induction on formulas
proves it correct. For $A$ use $\Xi$ (\cref{odg:def:thm:constants}) in place
of $\Std$, and the same four-square definition of $\N$, which is valid in $A$
because sums of squares are nonnegative in the ordered ring
$\mathcal A_\R(\Gamma)$. A recursive
axiomatization of a complete theory would decide each sentence by searching for
a proof of it or of its negation.
\end{proof}

For $\Oz$ the same holds sentence by sentence under the class convention.
Source 07 credits the non-axiomatizability of omnific arithmetic to
Je\v r\'abek's 2011 MathOverflow answer \cite{JerabekMO}, and source 06 cites
the same answer for the logical limitations of $\Oz$; the undecidability of
integer-coefficient solvability over $\Oz$ follows from transfer alone
(\cref{odg:cor:H10}). None of these consequences is presented as new, and none
is a new undecidability theorem about $\Q$ or about number fields.

\begin{theorem}[What equations cannot define \src{06, 07}]\label{odg:def:thm:collapse}
Let $\Gamma\ne0$, let $\mathfrak o\subseteq\Bbbk$ be a unital subring and
$\mathcal R=\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$. No positive-existential
formula with parameters in $\mathfrak o$ defines a subset of $\mathcal R$ that
contains a nonzero element of $\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$ and excludes $0$. In
particular $\mathcal R\setminus\{0\}$, $\mathcal R\setminus\mathfrak o$ and
$\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)\setminus\{0\}$ are not so definable, and in the real case
neither is the positive cone.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The composite $\mathcal R\xrightarrow{\ct}\mathfrak o\hookrightarrow
\mathcal R$ is an endomorphism fixing $\mathfrak o$, and positive-existential
formulas with parameters fixed by an endomorphism are preserved by it (as in
\cref{odg:thm:transfer}). If such a formula held at a nonzero
$a\in\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$, it would hold at the image $\ct(a)=0$. Each displayed set
contains $\omega^g$ for $g\in\Gamma^{>0}$, which is positive in the real case,
and excludes $0$.
\end{proof}

This generalizes \cref{odg:prop:notdiophantine}. It is consistent with the
Diophantine definitions of $\Pi$ and of $\{\ct\ne0\}$: the first contains $0$,
and the second contains no element of $\Pi$. The detector avoids the
obstruction precisely because it defines $\ct(a)\ne0$, not $a\ne0$: $\omega$
is nonzero but divides no value $\Lambda(t)$ \src{07}. The set
$\mathcal R\setminus\{0\}$ has the quantifier-free definition $x\ne0$; what
fails is a positive-existential definition. Source 07 left open whether $\Pi$
itself has one; \cref{odg:def:thm:ideal} of source 06 shows that it has.

\begin{proposition}[A quantifier-free lower bound \src{06}]\label{odg:def:prop:notqf}
In $\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ with $\Gamma\ne0$, neither $\Pi_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$ nor
$\Z$ is definable by a quantifier-free ring formula in one variable, even with
arbitrary parameters; the same holds for the ideal and $\Z[i]$ in
$\mathcal R_{\Z[i]}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$. Hence the complete theory of such a
set-sized ring does not eliminate quantifiers in the ring language.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
In an infinite domain a nonzero one-variable polynomial has finitely many
roots, so every quantifier-free one-variable definable set is finite or
cofinite. The ideal and $\mathfrak o$ are infinite, and so are their
complements: constants witness one, and the monomials $\omega^{kg}$, $k\ge1$,
the other.
\end{proof}

This is a first lower bound on logical complexity only; it does not show that
degree four, or five witnesses, is optimal \src{06}.

\begin{theorem}[Omitted types \src{07; also 06}]\label{odg:def:thm:saturation}
Let $A$ be a set-sized intermediate ring for $(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$, or a
set-sized ring as in \cref{odg:def:thm:numberfield}, and let $\Xi_A$ be the
corresponding constant-definition formula. The computable parameter-free type
\[
 \{\Xi_A(x)\}\cup\{Q(x)\ne0:0\ne Q\in\Z[X]\}
\]
is finitely satisfiable and omitted; so $A$ is not recursively saturated. For
the full rings $\Oz$ and $\Og$ the same explicit type is finitely satisfiable
and omitted.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A finite part excludes the roots of finitely many nonzero polynomials, which
are finitely many elements of the infinite ring $\mathfrak o$, so some element
of $\mathfrak o$ satisfies it. A realization of the whole type would lie in
$\mathfrak o$ and be algebraic over $\Q$, so some nonzero integer polynomial
would vanish at it.
\end{proof}

For $\mathfrak o=\Z$ the simpler countable type $\{\Std(x)\}\cup\{x\ne n:n\in\Z\}$
already shows that $\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ is not countably saturated, and
the Gaussian analogue holds with all Gaussian constants \src{06}. For the full
classes this is the class-relative formulation: no set-sized model carrier is
assumed. Nonsaturation of $\Oz$ has the 2018 precedent \cite{Nombre}. Even a
very rich exponent order does not make these rings recursively saturated; the
statement concerns the ring, not its order or a surrounding field \src{07}.

\begin{remark}[Transfer without elementarity \src{06; also 13}]\label{odg:def:rem:notelementary}
For $\Gamma\ne0$ the inclusion $\Z\hookrightarrow\mathcal R_\Z(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ is
not elementary, although it transfers positive-existential formulas: the
sentence $\exists x,y\,(x^2=2y^2\wedge y\ne0)$ is false in $\Z$ and in $\Z[i]$
and holds in the ring, with $y=\omega^g$ and $x=\sqrt2\,\omega^g$ for any
$g>0$ (when $\sqrt2\in\Bbbk$). This is a second route to the non-elementarity
recorded after \cref{odg:thm:induction} and in \cref{odg:rem:ringinduction}.
A formula that defines the actual integers in this ring need not select the
standard copy in an elementary extension, where it defines an elementary
extension of the induced arithmetic; this is compatible with the omitted
types above \src{06}.
\end{remark}

\section{Infinite families, primitivity, and projective points}\label{odg:sec:primitive}

\subsection{Polynomial curves through integer points}

\begin{proposition}[Finite polynomial lifting \src{01, 02, 05}]\label{odg:prop:polylift}
Let $F_1,\ldots,F_r\in\R[X_1,\ldots,X_n]$, and let
$P_1(T),\ldots,P_n(T)\in\R[T]$ satisfy
\[
 F_j(P_1(T),\ldots,P_n(T))=0\quad(1\leq j\leq r),
 \qquad P_i(0)\in\Z\quad(1\leq i\leq n).
\]
Then $\mathbf P(t)\in\Oz^n$ solves the system for every $t\in\Pi$, with
constant tuple $\mathbf P(0)$. If some $P_i$ is nonconstant, every
$t\in\Pi\setminus\{0\}$ produces an infinite point.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Every nonconstant term of $P_i(t)$ lies in $\Pi$, since $\Pi$ is an ideal and
a real vector space, so the constant coefficient is exactly $P_i(0)$.
Polynomial identities hold under substitution into any field extension. If
$P_i$ has degree $d>0$ and $\ddeg(t)=\gamma>0$, its top term has degree
$d\gamma$, strictly larger than every other term's degree.
\end{proof}

No integrality is required of the nonconstant coefficients: for example
$(1+t^2/2,\,t^2/2,\,t)$ is omnific for every $t\in\Pi$ although
$1/2\notin\Oz$. The finiteness of the polynomials matters, however.
Evaluating an infinite real power series at an infinite omnific argument is
not licensed by this proposition; the Taylor expansion of $\sqrt{1+T}$ is not
an admissible way to assert that $\sqrt{1+\omega}$ lies in $\Oz$. Polynomial
maps supply a systematic sufficient condition for nontrivial fibers; they do
not exhaust all normal-form constructions, and no such exhaustion is assumed
(but see \cref{odg:thm:specialization} for finite supports).

\subsection{Infinite solutions of homogeneous equations}

\begin{theorem}[Homogeneous cone criterion \src{01}; part (b) \src{02}]\label{odg:thm:cone}
Let $F_1,\ldots,F_r\in\R[X_1,\ldots,X_n]$ be homogeneous of positive
degrees.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)},leftmargin=*]
\item Their common zero locus has a nonzero point in $\Oz^n$ if and only
if it has a nonzero point in $\R^n$.
\item If the $F_j$ have ordinary integer coefficients, the
equivalence in (a) persists with any fixed combination of coordinate sign and
nonvanishing conditions preserved by positive scaling, and these existence
problems are decidable.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(a) A real nonzero common zero $\mathbf a$ gives the omnific common zero
$\omega\mathbf a\in\Pi^n$ by homogeneity. Conversely, take a nonzero common
zero $\mathbf x\in\Oz^n$. If every coordinate is constant, it is a real
point. Otherwise let $\gamma>0$ be the maximum of the coordinates' leading
degrees and $r_i=[\omega^\gamma]x_i$; the vector $\mathbf r$ is nonzero. For
$F_j$ homogeneous of degree $d_j$, the coefficient at $\omega^{d_j\gamma}$
in $F_j(\mathbf x)$ is $F_j(\mathbf r)$, since a product attains that exponent
only by taking exponent $\gamma$ in every factor. So $\mathbf r$ is a nonzero
real common zero. This route uses no quantifier elimination.

(b) A real solution $\mathbf r$ gives $\omega\mathbf r$, which has the same
signs and zero pattern. Conversely an omnific solution lies, with $\R$, in a
set-sized real closed subfield of $\No$ (generate a field and take its real
closure inside $\No$). Quantifier elimination makes the inclusion of $\R$
elementary \cite{Tarski}, so the existential statement with its finite sign
conditions already holds over $\R$; quantifier elimination is an effective
decision procedure for this finite formula with integer coefficients.
\end{proof}

The leading-vector route of (a) does not preserve sign or nonvanishing
conditions on coordinates of lower degree, which is why (b) uses Tarski. Any
homogeneous solution over $\No$, not only over $\R$, can be multiplied by a
common positive monomial into $\Pi^n$ preserving signs and zero patterns
(\cref{odg:cor:projectiveclear}) \src{02, 05}. Neither statement decides
affine equations: homogenizing an affine equation and requiring the new
coordinate to be nonzero allows division in $\No$, not in $\Oz$.

\begin{example}[Fermat scaling \src{01, 02, 05}]\label{odg:ex:fermatscaling}
For every ordinary integer $n\geq2$ and $0<t\in\Pi$,
\begin{equation}\label{odg:eq:fermatscaling}
 x=t,\qquad y=t,\qquad z=2^{1/n}t
\end{equation}
is a positive infinite solution of $x^n+y^n=z^n$; for $t=\omega$ this is
$\omega^n+\omega^n=(2^{1/n}\omega)^n$. The irrational coefficient is
permitted by the definition of $\Oz$. All constant coefficients are zero,
consistent with \cref{odg:thm:transfer}, whose specialization is $(0,0,0)$;
it does not refute Fermat's Last Theorem about nonzero ordinary integers. The
coordinates are divisible by every ordinary integer and have a common
infinite monomial divisor (\cref{odg:thm:commondivisor}), so these solutions
are not primitive and must not be cited as resolving a primitive Fermat
problem.
\end{example}

\status{Source 02 states that the failure of the nonzero analogue of
Fermat's Last Theorem for omnific integers ``is already known'', citing
comments by Emil Je\v r\'abek (27 June 2018) and Timothy Chow (16 November
2021) under MathOverflow answer 303688 \cite{MOFermat}. This attribution
could not be verified when this report was assembled (the site was not
reachable), and it is recorded as source 02's, unverified. No source claims
novelty for the example.}

\subsection{Unimodular Pythagorean triples}

\begin{proposition}[Infinite unimodular Pythagorean triples \src{01, 05}]\label{odg:prop:pythagoras}
For every $t\in\Pi$, put $x=t^2-1$, $y=2t$, $z=t^2+1$. Then
$x^2+y^2=z^2$ and the triple is unimodular. For $t\ne0$ all three
coordinates are infinite, and for $t>0$ they are positive.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The identity is the ordinary polynomial identity
$(t^2-1)^2+4t^2=(t^2+1)^2$. Two unit-ideal witnesses are
\begin{equation}\label{odg:eq:pythbezout}
 \frac{t^2}{2}z-\Bigl(\frac{t^2}{2}+1\Bigr)x=1\ \src{01},\qquad
 \frac t2\,y-x=1\ \src{05},
\end{equation}
with $t^2/2,\,t/2\in\Pi\subseteq\Oz$. Positive leading degree gives the
assertion about infinite size.
\end{proof}

The constant tuple is $(-1,0,1)$, not the zero tuple; that is exactly where
the common-divisor obstruction for $\Pi^3$ stops applying. Infinite
solutions therefore need not arise by scaling an ordinary tuple, and
exponent two differs from exponents at least three
(\cref{odg:thm:unimodularfermat}). More generally
$(u^2-v^2)^2+(2uv)^2=(u^2+v^2)^2$ for all $u,v\in\Oz$ \src{02}.

\subsection{Which real projective directions have primitive representatives?}

A point $[r_0:\cdots:r_n]\in\mathbb P^n(\R)$ is \emph{rational} if
it has homogeneous coordinates in $\Q$, equivalently a nonzero integer
coordinate tuple.

\begin{theorem}[Rationality of primitive constant directions \src{01}]\label{odg:thm:realray}
For a real projective point $p=[r_0:\cdots:r_n]$, the following are
equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=*]
\item $p$ is rational;
\item $p$ has a unimodular representative in $\Oz^{n+1}$;
\item $p$ has a primitive representative in $\Oz^{n+1}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For (i)$\Rightarrow$(ii), choose an integer representative with ordinary gcd
one; the integer B\'ezout identity makes it unimodular over $\Oz$.
(ii)$\Rightarrow$(iii) is immediate. For (iii)$\Rightarrow$(i), write a
primitive representative as $z_i=\lambda r_i$ with $\lambda\in\No^\times$
and choose $r_j\ne0$. Since $z_j\in\Oz\subseteq\BR$ and division by the
nonzero real $r_j$ preserves $\BR$, $\lambda\in\BR$. Put $c=\ct(\lambda)$;
then $\ct(z_i)=cr_i\in\Z$ for all $i$. If $c=0$, every $z_i$ lies in $\Pi$,
so $2$ divides every coordinate, contradicting primitivity. Hence $c\ne0$ and
$(cr_0,\ldots,cr_n)$ is a nonzero integer representative of $p$.
\end{proof}

For example, $[1:\sqrt2]$ has omnific representatives such as
$(\omega,\sqrt2\,\omega)$ but no primitive, hence no unimodular,
representative; source 02 proved the unimodular half of this example
directly. The theorem concerns real projective points; it is not a
classification of all points of $\mathbb P^n(\No)$, and neither monomial
clearing nor the existence of a homogeneous solution answers the
primitive-representative question.

\subsection{Finite supports specialize to polynomial arcs}

The rigidity theorems above do not depend on finite supports. Finite-support
expressions nevertheless admit an additional reduction \src{05}.

\begin{lemma}[Finite ordered specialization \src{05}]\label{odg:lem:specialization}
Let $H$ be a finitely generated subgroup of an ordered abelian group and
$E\subseteq H$ finite. There is an additive map $\ell:H\to\Z$ with
$\ell(a)>\ell(b)$ whenever $a,b\in E$ and $a>b$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A finitely generated torsion-free abelian group is isomorphic to $\Z^r$. In
such coordinates, form the finite set of vectors $a-b$ for $a,b\in E$ with
$a>b$; they are positive in the original order. Zero is not in their convex
hull in $\R^r$: otherwise, since the vectors are integral, the rational
linear system describing such a convex combination would have a rational
solution (a nonempty polytope given by rational equations, nonnegativity and
a coordinate-sum equation has a rational vertex, obtained by setting a
maximal set of coordinates to zero and solving the remaining independent
rational equations), and clearing denominators would give a nontrivial sum of
positive group elements equal to zero. Strict separation of this compact
convex set from zero gives a real linear functional positive on all these
vectors; a small rational perturbation keeps the finitely many strict
inequalities, and clearing denominators gives an integer-valued functional.
If there are no strict comparisons, $\ell=0$ works.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite-support solutions specialize to arcs \src{05}]\label{odg:thm:specialization}
Suppose $\mathbf x\in\Oz^n$ has finite normal-form supports and satisfies a
finite polynomial system over $\Z$. There are real polynomials
$f_1(T),\ldots,f_n(T)$ satisfying the same polynomial identities with
$f_i(0)=\ct(x_i)$, and every nonconstant $x_i$ gives a nonconstant $f_i$. A
prescribed finite list of nonzero polynomial expressions in $\mathbf x$ can
be kept nonzero, with the same eventual signs as $T\to+\infty$ as their
surreal signs.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $E$ contain zero, all exponents of the coordinates, and all exponents of
the prescribed expressions after expansion; it is finite. Let $H$ be its
integer span and choose $\ell$ by \cref{odg:lem:specialization}. The
group-algebra map $\R[H]\to\R[T,T^{-1}]$, $\omega^h\mapsto T^{\ell(h)}$, is a
ring homomorphism. Positive coordinate exponents map to positive integers,
so coordinate images are polynomials with the same constant coefficients;
injectivity of $\ell$ on $E$ prevents cancellation of distinct supported
terms, in particular of leading terms. Polynomial identities are preserved,
and each prescribed expression keeps its leading coefficient and hence its
eventual sign for large positive $T$.
\end{proof}

With \cref{odg:prop:polylift} (substituting $t\in\Pi$ for $T$), this gives an
exact equivalence between the existence of a finite-support nonconstant
solution and of a nonconstant real polynomial arc with integer constant
coordinates. It does not turn infinite-support solutions into polynomial
arcs.

For a finite-support implementation one may store an element as a map from
exact exponents to nonzero exact real coefficients; the essential operations
are coefficientwise addition, exponent addition in convolution, combination
of equal exponents, and deciding integrality of the constant coefficient.
Rational exponents with real algebraic coefficients give a practical
decidable representation; general symbolic reals or arbitrary surreal
exponents need further equality and order oracles. Useful exact certificates
are polynomial identities, B\'ezout witnesses such as \eqref{odg:eq:pythbezout},
standard-modulus quotients, and finite normal-form obstructions such as the
first negative term in \eqref{odg:eq:binomial} \src{05}.

\section{Equations with omnific coefficients}\label{odg:sec:coefficients}

For $P\in\Oz[X_1,\ldots,X_n]$ let $\overline P\in\Z[X_1,\ldots,X_n]$ apply
$\ct$ to every coefficient. Then \src{02, 05}
\[
 \ct(P(\mathbf x))=\overline P(\ct x_1,\ldots,\ct x_n),\qquad
 P(\mathbf x)=0\ \Longrightarrow\ \overline P(\ct\mathbf x)=0 .
\]
The reverse implication need not hold, because the coefficients are no longer
fixed by $\ct$.

\subsection{Quadratic equations}

\begin{theorem}[Square-discriminant criterion \src{02}]\label{odg:thm:quadraticformula}
For $b,c\in\Oz$, the equation $x^2+bx+c=0$ has a root in $\Oz$ if and only
if $\Delta=b^2-4c$ is a square in $\Oz$. If $y^2=\Delta$ with $y\in\Oz$, its
roots are $(-b\pm y)/2$, and both are omnific.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A root gives $(2x+b)^2=\Delta$. Conversely, suppose $y^2=\Delta$.
Specializing and reducing modulo $4$ gives $\ct(y)^2\equiv\ct(b)^2\pmod4$.
Integer squares are $0$ or $1$ modulo $4$, so $\ct(y)$ and $\ct(b)$ have the
same parity, and \cref{odg:thm:finitequotients} shows that $-b\pm y$ are
divisible by two in $\Oz$. The usual identity verifies the roots.
\end{proof}

Testing whether a nonnegative surreal has an omnific square root is not the
same as testing whether it has a surreal square root: real closedness gives
the latter, while nonnegative support and an integral constant must still be
checked for the former.

\begin{example}[Residual and leading tests that do not lift \src{01, 02, 05}]\label{odg:ex:nonsquare}
The equation $x^2=\omega^2+1$ has no solution in $\Oz$
(\cref{odg:prop:notnormal}). Nevertheless its residual equation
$\ct(x)^2=1$ is solvable, and the leading terms allow degree $1$ and leading
coefficient $\pm1$. The positive root in $\No$ has the formal binomial
expansion
\[
 \sqrt{\omega^2+1}
 =\omega+\tfrac12\omega^{-1}-\tfrac18\omega^{-3}
   +\tfrac1{16}\omega^{-5}-\cdots,
\]
whose negative exponents exclude it from $\Oz$. This is a support-controlled
Hahn expansion, not a claim of convergence in the surreal order topology. In
contrast, $\omega^2+2\omega+1=(\omega+1)^2$ is an omnific square.
\end{example}

The sibling report \fn{docs/surreal/set-sized-quotients-of-omnific-integers}
prints a later manuscript's image-gap theorem at a fresh scale (its files
carry the prefix \fn{16-fresh-scale-}): if $f$ has degree at least two and
coefficients in the Hahn field $K_G$ with exponents in a set-sized group $G$,
and $t=\omega^b$ with $b>G$, then $f(\BR)\cap(f(t)+K_G)=\{f(t)\}$, and
likewise over $\BC$; for ordinary $f$ one may take $G=\{0\}$ and any $b>0$.
The unsolvability of $x^2=\omega^2+1$ in \cref{odg:ex:nonsquare} is the case
$f=X^2$, $t=\omega$, and the two-term equations of \cref{odg:prop:binomial}
are the cases $f=aY^n$, $t=\omega^{\gamma/n}$, after the substitution
$X=a^{1/n}Y$ in $\BR$. The short proofs here are kept; the general statement
is printed there.

\subsection{Initial forms}

\begin{proposition}[Initial forms of an omnific solution \src{01, 02}]\label{odg:prop:initial}
Let $P=\sum_\alpha a_\alpha\mathbf X^\alpha\in\Oz[X_1,\ldots,X_n]$ be nonzero,
let $\mathbf x\in\Oz^n$ have all coordinates nonzero, and write
$\gamma_i=\ddeg(x_i)$, $r_i=\lc(x_i)$. Discarding zero coefficients, put
\[
 W=\max_\alpha\Bigl(\ddeg(a_\alpha)+\sum_i\alpha_i\gamma_i\Bigr).
\]
If $P(\mathbf x)=0$, then
\begin{equation}\label{odg:eq:initial}
 \sum_{\ddeg(a_\alpha)+\sum_i\alpha_i\gamma_i=W}
       \lc(a_\alpha)\prod_i r_i^{\alpha_i}=0,
\end{equation}
and in particular the maximum is attained by at least two monomials.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{odg:lem:degree}, each evaluated monomial has degree
$\ddeg(a_\alpha)+\sum_i\alpha_i\gamma_i$ and leading coefficient
$\lc(a_\alpha)\prod_ir_i^{\alpha_i}$. The coefficient of $P(\mathbf x)$ at
$\omega^W$ is exactly the sum in \eqref{odg:eq:initial}, and it must vanish; a
single maximizer would contribute a nonzero product.
\end{proof}

Variables assigned zero can first be substituted out. For ordinary
coefficients this is a weighted initial polynomial over $\R$ with surreal
weights. The constant-coefficient equation and the initial equation constrain
opposite ends of a normal form; they are complementary necessary tests, not a
lifting criterion, as \cref{odg:ex:nonsquare} shows: the missing information
lies in the full tail of a potential root.

\subsection{Two-term roots and failed lifting}

\begin{proposition}[A two-term root obstruction \src{05}]\label{odg:prop:binomial}
Let $a\in\R_{>0}$, $\gamma>0$, $b\in\Z$, and let $n\ge2$ be ordinary. The
equation $X^n=a\omega^\gamma+b$ has a solution in $\Oz$ if and only if $b=0$.
When $b=0$ its positive solution is $a^{1/n}\omega^{\gamma/n}$, together with
its negative when $n$ is even.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The right side is positive and infinite. Its positive $n$th root in $\No$ has
the formal binomial expansion
\begin{equation}\label{odg:eq:binomial}
 a^{1/n}\omega^{\gamma/n}
 \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{1/n}{k}
 \Bigl(\frac ba\Bigr)^k\omega^{-k\gamma},
\end{equation}
whose exponents strictly decrease; the formal power-series binomial identity
shows it is an $n$th root in the Hahn field, positivity gives uniqueness among
positive roots, and in an ordered field the only other real root is its
negative when $n$ is even. If $b\ne0$, the $k=1$ coefficient is nonzero at
the exponent $\gamma/n-\gamma<0$, which no other term has, so neither root is
omnific. If $b=0$ the root is the single positive monomial displayed.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[A simple residue root need not lift \src{05}]\label{odg:ex:hensel}
Let $f(T)=T^2-T-\omega$. Modulo $\Pi$ it becomes $T(T-1)$, and the residue
root $0$ is simple, with derivative $-1$, a unit. Nevertheless $f$ has no root
in $\Oz$: its roots in $\No$ are
\[
 \tfrac12\pm\bigl(\omega^{1/2}+\tfrac18\omega^{-1/2}
 -\tfrac1{128}\omega^{-3/2}+\cdots\bigr),
\]
with constant coefficient $\tfrac12$ and negative exponents. Hensel-style
intuition fails for $\Oz\to\Z$: the kernel $\Pi$ is not contained in the
Jacobson radical, since $-\omega\in\Pi$ but $1+\omega$ is not a unit, and
$\Pi^2=\Pi$, so there is no decreasing nilpotent-error filtration. This is a
failure of the simple-root lifting property itself.
\end{example}

For general coefficients, a viable approach must inspect actual normal-form
supports. Matching constant terms is necessary, and occasionally useful, but
is not a sufficient solvability criterion (\cref{odg:q:roots}).

\section{Fixed Hahn workspaces and a formalization route}\label{odg:sec:formal}

\subsection{Which results are local in the exponent group?}

For a set-sized ordered abelian group $\Gamma$, work in a Hahn field whose
monomials are written $\omega^\gamma$ with reverse-well-ordered supports, and
define \src{01, 02}
\[
 \Oz_\Gamma=\Z+\Bigl\{\sum_{\gamma>0}r_\gamma\omega^\gamma\Bigr\},
 \qquad
 \mathcal B_\Gamma=\R+\Bigl\{\sum_{\gamma>0}r_\gamma\omega^\gamma\Bigr\}.
\]
The displayed sums are elements of the ambient Hahn field with the specified
support restrictions; this is a convention, not an identification of every
abstract group element with a surreal exponent. When an ordered embedding of
$\Gamma$ into $\No$ is fixed, it gives the corresponding surreal workspace.

\paragraph{Local results.} The ring decompositions, the unit lemma, the
retraction, finite quotients, equational transfer, the Smith criterion,
constant-product and decomposable rigidity, binary, Pell and norm rigidity,
the quadratic constructions, the quartic guards, and all explicit
polynomial identities apply to $\Oz_\Gamma$ \src{01, 02}. Their proofs use finite algebra,
leading exponents and real coefficients, not the availability of a new
exponent outside $\Gamma$. The derivation arguments of
\cref{odg:sec:derivations} take place in the workspace of the rational span of
the relevant supports, so they apply to $\Oz_\Gamma$ after passing to the
divisible hull of $\Gamma$ \src{05}. If $\Gamma\ne0$, its positive cone
provides nonconstant parameters for the infinite-family statements; the case
$\Gamma=0$ is $\Oz_\Gamma=\Z$. The Lorentz family uses only $t$, $t^2$ and
real scalars, so one positive monomial already produces examples. If the
coefficient field is restricted below $\R$, the general indefinite lifting
additionally needs the chosen real vectors $e,u$ to be available, or an
explicit scalar extension \src{02}. The definability results of sources 06
and 07 (\cref{odg:def:sec:arithmetic,odg:def:sec:ct}) and the multiplier
identity are proved for an arbitrary ordered exponent group, so they apply to
$\Oz_\Gamma=\mathcal R_\Z(\R,\Gamma)$ as stated; the multiplier identity needs
$\Gamma\ne0$. Only $\Frac(\Oz)=\No$ and the reconstruction of $\No$
(\cref{odg:def:thm:realrecovery}) use the full class, and the fraction field
of $\Oz_\Gamma$ need not be the whole Hahn field \src{06}.

\paragraph{Full-class results.} The common-divisor theorem for an arbitrary
set of purely infinite elements, the monomial clearing theorem, $\Pi^2=\Pi$,
the non-generation of $\Pi$ by a set, $\Frac(\Oz)=\No$, the general pair
without a gcd (\cref{odg:thm:nogcd}) and the set-sized quotient theorem
(\cref{odg:fact:settarget}) require bounds that may not exist in $\Gamma$.
For instance, in $\Gamma=\R$ the set $\{1/k:k\geq1\}$ has no strictly
positive real lower bound, whereas in $\No$ it has one. A support in $\Gamma$
may also be unbounded below in $\Gamma$. These conclusions are not asserted
for $\Oz_\Gamma$; in particular one must not infer $\Pi_\Gamma^2=\Pi_\Gamma$ by
copying the proof for $\No$ \src{01, 02, 05}. Proofs that stay inside a
workspace must be distinguished from proofs that enlarge it.

\subsection{A formalization route}

No part of this report has been formalized. At the placement commit the
repository contains no Lean declaration about omnific integers. The sources
proposed overlapping routes; the following merges them. All module
names are suggestions, not claims that such files or declarations exist.

\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.3\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.6\textwidth}}
\toprule
Proposed layer & Main obligations\\
\midrule
\fn{Omnific.Basic} \src{01, 02, 05} & In a real Hahn field over a
set-sized ordered divisible group: the nonnegative-support subring, the
integer-constant subring, the retraction $\ct$ from the zero-exponent
convolution formula, and the finite-element criterion.\\
\fn{Omnific.Residue} \src{01, 02} & $n\Oz=\Pi+n\Z$, finite quotients,
preservation of $\Pi$; source 02 suggests proving these once for any ring
retraction onto $\Z$ with an integer-divisible kernel.\\
\fn{Omnific.Rigidity} \src{01, 02, 05} & Additive leading degree, units of
the real and complex support rings, the exact decomposable fibers (finite
linear algebra plus coefficient extensionality), binary and norm rigidity.\\
\fn{Omnific.Derivation} \src{05} & Euler derivations from a $\Q$-linear
functional (Leibniz from finite convolution fibers), the quotient step
for separated powers, comaximality of powers, and Wronskian degree bounds.\\
\fn{Omnific.Quadratic} \src{01, 02} & The finite bilinear identities for
$U_t$ over any field of characteristic zero, the isotropic-vector choices,
and the level classification.\\
\fn{Omnific.IntegralityGuard} \src{01, 05} & Pell rigidity with
\fn{Nat.sum_four_squares} (source 05's six-auxiliary form); source 01's
five-auxiliary form needs a three-square theorem, which Mathlib lacks.\\
\fn{Omnific.Definability} \src{06, 07} & Units of $\mathcal A_\Bbbk(\Gamma)$
on the large-monomial side (a small-monomial Hahn implementation must reverse
the exponent sign), Pell rigidity by coefficient extension, the finite
Pell-divisibility argument, intersectivity of $\Lambda$ with every prime power
including $2$, $13$ and $17$ (a finite modular computation is no substitute),
the order-free system, the augmentation root detector (a statement about
augmented commutative algebras, independent of surreals), the multiplier
identity, and then the transport to $\Oz$ and $\Og$.\\
\fn{Omnific.Global} \src{01, 02, 05} & The small-cut theorem for support
bounds, common monomial divisibility and global clearing, kept separate and
with explicit smallness (universe) hypotheses; the upper bound for clearing
and the positive lower bound for idempotence are distinct lemmas.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Source 05 points to the normal-form bridge (\fn{docs/NORMAL_FORM_BRIDGE.md})
and the modules \fn{SmallNormalFormFieldEquiv.lean},
\fn{SmallNormalFormHahnEmbedding.lean},
\fn{SmallNormalFormWorkspace.lean} and \fn{SignSequenceWorkspace.lean}
under \fn{Surreal/Foundations/} as the transfer route to the actual
surreal carrier; all four exist at the placement commit (with
\fn{SignSequenceWorkspaceExponents.lean}), and their signatures must be
checked before use. The imported logical theorems (Tarski's quantifier
elimination, MRDP) remain separate dependencies; formalizing the retraction
does not formalize them. The transvection identity is finite bilinear
algebra with no dependence on birthday recursion, and the only hard
number-theoretic input to the guards is the three- or four-square theorem.
The order-free system needs only elementary local arithmetic of $\Lambda$ and
the finite Pell argument; the number-field extension also needs Dirichlet's
theorem and quadratic reciprocity, and the reconstruction of $\No$ the real
closedness of $\No$. Sources 06 and 07 propose the order followed in the row
\fn{Omnific.Definability}, keeping the class-sized instantiation separate from
the set-sized Hahn results; this is a proposal, not a report of completed
formalization.

\section{Questions and synthesis}\label{odg:sec:questions}

The sources list continuation problems that their proofs do not decide. None
of them claims that these problems are open in the literature, or that the
list is exhaustive. Two were settled inside the batch of manuscripts from
which sources 01, 02 and 05 come. In the merge of sources 06 and 07 one more
was answered and two were answered in part.

\subsection{Questions settled by source 05}\label{odg:sub:settled}

\paragraph{Coprime exponents and $Y^2=X^3+1$ (source 01).} Source 01 asked
which curves such as $Y^2=X^3+1$ have genuinely infinite omnific points, and
proved rigidity of $x^m-y^n=c$ only for $\gcd(m,n)>1$
(\cref{odg:rem:powers}). \Cref{odg:thm:separated} of source 05 answers this
for every separated equation $ax^m+by^n=c$ with $abc\ne0$ and $m,n\ge2$; in
particular $Y^2=X^3+1$ and $x^2-y^3=1$ have only ordinary omnific points
(\cref{odg:cor:mordell}). The general part of source 01's question, a
criterion for arbitrary affine curves, remains open as
\cref{odg:q:affine}.

\paragraph{Unimodular Fermat (sources 01 and 02).} Source 01 asked for the
classification of primitive or unimodular omnific solutions of
$X^n+Y^n=Z^n$ with nonzero coordinates, $n\ge3$; source 02 asked which
homogeneous systems admit nonzero omnific solutions generating $\Oz$.
\Cref{odg:thm:unimodularfermat,odg:cor:flt} of source 05 settle the
unimodular Fermat case: there are none. The primitive case (no common nonunit
divisor) remains open as \cref{odg:q:primitivefermat}, and source 02's
question for other homogeneous systems remains open as
\cref{odg:q:homogeneous}.

\subsection{Questions answered in the merge of sources 06 and 07}\label{odg:def:sub:settled}

\paragraph{An existential definition of $\Pi$ (source 01; source 07).} Source
01 asked for an existential definition of $\Pi$ over $\Oz$
(\cref{odg:q:existentialPi}), and source 07 closed by asking whether the purely
infinite ideal, or the graph of $\ct$, has a positive-existential definition.
Source 06 answers both: $\exists y\,(x^2=2y^2)$ defines $\Pi$, with one witness
and no parameters, in $\Oz$, in $\Og$ and in every ring
$\mathcal R_{\mathfrak o}(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$ with $\sqrt2\in\Bbbk$ and
$\sqrt2\notin\Frac(\mathfrak o)$ (\cref{odg:def:thm:ideal}), and the graph of
$\ct$ is Diophantine (\cref{odg:def:thm:ctgraph}). Source 07's detector makes
the complement $\{\ct\ne0\}$ Diophantine too (\cref{odg:def:thm:detector}). The
second part of \cref{odg:q:existentialPi}, which fibers or subideals of $\Pi$
are Diophantine, remains open.

\paragraph{The least degree of a definition of $\Z$ (source 06).} Source 06
asked for the least total degree, and the best trade-off between degree and
witnesses, of a single integer polynomial defining $\Z$ in $\Oz$, offering a
quintic with seven witnesses. The guards of sources 01 and 05
(\cref{odg:thm:guard,odg:thm:standarddef}), which source 06 did not see, reach
degree four with five and six witnesses. This answers the question in part;
the least degree and the trade-off remain open and are merged into
\cref{odg:q:guard}, together with source 06's question on a single Gaussian
polynomial and source 07's on better quantifier and degree bounds.

\subsection{Open questions}

\begin{question}[Rigid affine varieties and curves \src{01, 02, 05}]\label{odg:q:affine}
For which affine varieties $X$ over $\Z$ does $X(\Oz)=X(\Z)$ hold? For which
affine curves does every omnific point have ordinary coordinates? A natural
next test is $Y^2=X^3+aX+b$ with $a\ne0$, outside
\cref{odg:thm:separated}.
\end{question}
\Cref{odg:thm:decomposable,odg:thm:binary,odg:thm:separated} answer this for
nonzero decomposable levels with trivial common kernel and for separated
equations, \cref{odg:thm:bounded,odg:thm:infinity} for bounded loci and
systems with no real point at infinity, and \cref{odg:thm:quadclassification}
and polynomial arcs give large opposing classes. A satisfactory criterion
should separate polynomial parametrization, torus rigidity and genuinely
algebraic branches \src{01}; it needs either a new degree inequality or a
structural analysis of possible supports \src{05}. No claim is made that all
other fibers are rigid, or that all nontrivial fibers come from real
polynomial curves \src{02}.

\begin{question}[Primitive Fermat triples \src{01, 05}]\label{odg:q:primitivefermat}
For $n\ge3$, can the unit-ideal hypothesis of
\cref{odg:thm:unimodularfermat} be weakened to ``no common nonunit
divisor''? That is, are there primitive, non-unimodular omnific solutions of
$X^n+Y^n=Z^n$ with nonzero coordinates?
\end{question}
The answer cannot be read off from ordinary integer intuition; it needs an
explicit relation between common divisors, ideal generation and the
applicable factorization theory. \Cref{odg:thm:unimodularfermat} deliberately
avoids any such relation, and the binary nonzero-fiber theorem does not
directly answer a three-variable homogeneous question \src{01}.

\begin{question}[Primitive homogeneous solutions \src{02}]\label{odg:q:homogeneous}
Which homogeneous systems admit a nonzero omnific solution whose coordinates
generate $\Oz$, and how does this depend on the chosen integral model of the
projective variety?
\end{question}
The real-solvability criterion \cref{odg:thm:cone} omits unimodularity, and
\cref{odg:thm:realray} exhibits the gap. A gcd normalization cannot be
assumed, by \cref{odg:thm:nogcd}. Fermat equations of exponent at least
three have none (\cref{odg:cor:flt}); the exponent-two Pythagorean and
indefinite quadratic cases have many (\cref{odg:prop:pythagoras},
\eqref{odg:eq:hyperunimodular}).

\begin{question}[An existential definition of $\Pi$ \src{01}; first part answered by \src{06}]\label{odg:q:existentialPi}
Is there an existential definition of $\Pi$ over $\Oz$ with ordinary integer
parameters? More generally, which fibers or subideals of $\Pi$ are
Diophantine? Retraction stability is necessary but far from sufficient.
\end{question}
The first question is answered: $\exists y\,(x^2=2y^2)$ defines $\Pi$
(\cref{odg:def:thm:ideal}, \cref{odg:def:sub:settled}). The second remains
open. Retraction stability is still necessary
(\cref{odg:def:thm:collapse}): a Diophantine set containing a nonzero element
of $\Pi$ contains $0$.

\begin{question}[Complexity of the integrality guard \src{01, 06, 07}]\label{odg:q:guard}
Can the number of auxiliary variables be reduced below five, with a single
quartic equation, or can a meaningful lower bound be proved? What is the least
total degree of a single integer polynomial defining $\Z$ in $\Oz$, and what
is the best trade-off between degree and witnesses \src{06}? Can the Gaussian
three-equation system of \cref{odg:def:thm:constants} be replaced by a single
polynomial of controlled degree \src{06}, and can its quantifier and degree
bounds be improved without an order \src{07}? The present constructions give
explicit upper bounds only (five in \cref{odg:thm:guard}, six in
\cref{odg:thm:standarddef}, seven in the quintic
\eqref{odg:def:eq:quintic}); other rigid arithmetic families might serve as
finite bounds.
\end{question}
Any Gaussian sum-of-squares replacement must address isotropy
($1^2+i^2=0$) rather than assume it away \src{06}. The quantifier-free
obstruction \cref{odg:def:prop:notqf} is only a first lower bound on logical
complexity; it does not make any of the present counts optimal.

\begin{question}[Roots with omnific coefficients \src{01, 02, 05}]\label{odg:q:roots}
For polynomials with finitely represented omnific coefficients, which root
questions in a chosen support class are decidable? Can one give tractable
necessary and sufficient support criteria for a root of a polynomial in
$\Oz[T]$ to be omnific?
\end{question}
\Cref{odg:thm:quadraticformula} reduces the monic quadratic case to square
membership, \cref{odg:prop:binomial} settles two-term binomials, and
\cref{odg:ex:hensel} rules out a naive lifting theorem; multiterm
cancellation needs separate analysis. Any effective claim must specify the
coefficients, the exponent representation, and whether workspace enlargement
is permitted. An unrestricted algorithm for ordinary-coefficient affine
existence would contradict \cref{odg:cor:H10}.

\begin{question}[The exact size boundary \src{05}]\label{odg:q:size}
Which set-sized exponent groups or universe-relative constructions admit
analogues of the common-monomial and set-sized quotient theorems?
\end{question}
The sibling report \fn{docs/surreal/set-sized-quotients-of-omnific-integers}
contains cardinal-threshold results on this question for specific set-sized
integer parts.

\begin{question}[Effective finite-support discovery \src{05}]\label{odg:q:search}
How far can a search for polynomial arcs and exact B\'ezout certificates,
suggested by \cref{odg:thm:specialization}, go before transfinite support
constructions are needed? It can find witnesses and expose obstructions, but
it cannot become a complete decision procedure for integer-coefficient
systems, by \cref{odg:cor:H10}.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Coefficient recovery of lower complexity \src{06}]\label{odg:def:q:coeffpe}
Can the canonical $\R$ or $\C$ in the interpreted fraction field be recovered
with weaker quantifier complexity than the multiplier formula of
\cref{odg:def:cor:internal}, keeping the integer-part ring as the only named
structure?
\end{question}
The pure field and the field with its integer-part predicate must be
distinguished in formulating this. The present formulas are first order in
the interpreted ring structure; they give no Diophantine definition of $\Z$ in
$\Q$ and no solution of Hilbert's tenth problem over $\Q$ \src{06}.

\begin{question}[Which real structure must be added? \src{06}]\label{odg:def:q:realform}
What additional natural structure on $\Og$ rules out the coefficient-fixing
twists of \cref{odg:def:lem:twist} and determines the real omnific subring,
without simply naming conjugation or $\Oz$?
\end{question}
Naming all complex constants does not suffice
(\cref{odg:def:thm:norealaxis}); naming a suitable involution does, but
gives the real structure explicitly rather than reconstructing it \src{06}.

\begin{question}[Infinite algebraic extensions \src{07}]\label{odg:def:q:algebraic}
Which coefficient subrings of algebraic extensions of $\Q$ of infinite degree
admit a root-free intersective polynomial, or another positive-existential
certificate of a nonzero constant, so that
\cref{odg:def:thm:numberfield} extends to them?
\end{question}
No polynomial of the shape used in \cref{odg:def:lem:tailored} is root-free in
a field containing all algebraic numbers, so a different certificate would be
needed there \src{07}.

\subsection{Synthesis}

The arithmetic of $\Oz$ is governed by three simultaneous facts:
\[
 \Oz/\Pi=\Z,\qquad \Oz^\times=\{\pm1\},\qquad\Frac(\Oz)=\No.
\]
The first makes ordinary congruences and equation-existence questions look
classical. The second imposes powerful rigidity on nonzero product fibers.
The third makes projective representation flexible while saying little about
primitive representatives. Growth degree, support and chosen Euler
derivations then govern whether individual solutions escape the ordinary
integers \src{05}.

For Diophantine equations the key divide is not only between finite and
infinite numbers. It is between \emph{equation existence}, which the
retraction controls; \emph{multiplicative, differential or bounded
rigidity}, which can force every coordinate to be ordinary; and
\emph{polynomial flexibility}, which creates infinite points without any
infinitesimal tail \src{02}. The difference between a Pell conic and a
three-variable indefinite quadric is structural: binary factorization forces
constants, while an additional indefinite dimension supports finite
polynomial isometries. The guards use the rigid side of this dichotomy to
recover the ordinary integers inside $\Oz$. Omnific integers preserve much
more of ordinary equational existence than their size suggests, and much less
of ordinary quantified arithmetic than their discreteness suggests
\src{05}. None of this requires unique factorization, a Euclidean termination
principle, or Conway's refinement property.

Sources 06 and 07 show that the decomposition itself is visible from inside
the ring. The ideal $\Pi$ is the solution set of $x^2=2y^2$, its complement is
the set of divisors of values of $\Lambda$, and $\Z$ is cut out by a Pell
equation, a divisibility and an intersective certificate; so the retraction
$\ct$, although not a symbol of the language, has a Diophantine graph. The
multiplier ring of $\Pi$ then recovers the real coefficients, and with them the
valued field $\No$, from the pure ring $\Oz$, while the Gaussian ring $\Og$
recovers $\C$ but not its real form. Arithmetic recovery and recovery of a real
form are different tasks \src{06}.

\subsection{Relations to neighbouring reports}\label{odg:sub:neighbours}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item \fn{docs/foundations-and-computation/foundations}: defines
$\Oz=\Pi\oplus\Z$ (\replabel{found:eq:omnific}, subsection
\replabel{found:sub:omnific}) with the same letter $\Pi$; this report develops
the arithmetic of that ring.
\item \fn{docs/surcomplex/trigonometry}: writes the purely infinite part
with $t$-exponents of the opposite sign (\replabel{trigonometry:eq:split};
translation in \cref{odg:sub:notation}); its integer-part construction agrees
with \cref{odg:thm:floor}, and its identity
$2\pi\Oz=\Pi+2\pi\Z$ (\replabel{trigonometry:eq:periodclass}) rests on the same
fact $r\Pi=\Pi$ for real $r\ne0$ as \cref{odg:thm:finitequotients}.
\item \fn{docs/surcomplex/gamma-and-zeta-functions}: the parity statements
of \replabel{gz:cor:canonical-zeros} ($-\omega/2\in\Oz$, $-\omega-1\notin2\Oz$)
are instances of \cref{odg:thm:finitequotients}; its statement that the
internal integers of an ultrapower are not $\Oz$ is consistent with
\cref{odg:thm:induction,odg:rem:ringinduction}.
\item \fn{docs/surreal/transcendence-over-bounded-support}: its
hypothesis \replabel{bst:hyp:gcd} that the bounded-support ring is a GCD
domain concerns a different ring, whose units are the nonzero monomials;
\cref{odg:thm:nogcd} concerns $\Oz$, whose units are $\pm1$. Neither statement
bears on the other.
\item \fn{docs/surreal/set-sized-quotients-of-omnific-integers}: prints the
universal set-sized quotient theorem (\cref{odg:fact:settarget}) with source
05's proof and further threshold results; its preliminaries overlap
\cref{odg:sec:basic,odg:sec:residues,odg:sec:class}. Its manuscript 13
proves the Gaussian fiber theorem, the kernel converse and \'etale norm
rigidity printed here once in \cref{odg:sec:rigidity}; its manuscript 16
proves the fresh-scale image-gap theorem of which the equations of
\cref{odg:ex:nonsquare,odg:prop:binomial} are special cases.
\Cref{odg:def:thm:homct} concerns homomorphisms into class-sized Hahn rings as
well and is consistent with the quotient theorem there.
\item \fn{docs/surcomplex/surcomplex-field-automorphisms}: constructs the
coefficient-fixing phase twists and the dilations $\mathsf S_a$; source 06
credits it and adds that the twist of \cref{odg:def:lem:twist} preserves $\Og$,
whence \cref{odg:def:thm:norealaxis}.
\item \fn{docs/surcomplex/single-dilation-hahn-support}: shows that the single
automorphism $S_2$ of a Hahn field defines its coefficient field, constant
coefficient and valuation ring in the language of fields with one
automorphism; \cref{odg:def:thm:multiplier,odg:def:thm:realrecovery} recover the
coefficient field, order and valuation ring from the pure ring $\Oz$ and its
Diophantine ideal instead.
\item \fn{docs/surreal/euclidean-three-space}: its theorem that class
homomorphisms from $\SO(3,\No)$ to set-sized groups factor through the
standard part (\replabel{e3:cut:thm:smallquotient}) is an analogous size
phenomenon for groups, not the same theorem; by \cref{odg:cor:orthogonal},
$\SO(3,\Oz)$ is finite.
\end{itemize}

\section{Provenance and merge record}\label{odg:app:provenance}

\subsection{Sources}

The report is built from five manuscripts. Sources 01, 02 and 05 are three of
a batch of eleven placed in commit \texttt{be06fc8}, and their numbers are the
batch item numbers; they are dated September 22, 2026, and the repository
commit they pin is \pin. Sources 06 and 07 are items 01 and 07 of a batch of
eight placed in commit \texttt{cf350b1}, numbered here after the earlier
sources, as their shipped file prefixes are; they are dated September 23,
2026, and the commit they pin is
\nolinkurl{71e9606d297c9b98c732070dc462682cc6948981} (``Prove Prony's main
theorem, holonomic ODE rigidity and eight more results''), which precedes
\texttt{be06fc8}. None of the five was aware of the others. Item 03 of the
second batch is manuscript 13 of the sibling report; its Gaussian and \'etale
fiber results (its Sections 8 and 9) are printed here, tagged \src{13}, and
its remaining results there. The manuscripts themselves are not shipped.
Their code and data are shipped under the prefixes
\fn{01-diophantine-geometry-}, \fn{02-diophantine-},
\fn{05-diophantine-rigidity-}, \fn{06-definability-reconstruction-} and
\fn{07-defining-arithmetic-}; source 02's provenance note, source 05's build
note and source 06's source audit are shipped as
\fn{02-diophantine-PROVENANCE.md}, \fn{05-diophantine-rigidity-BUILD.md} and
\fn{06-definability-reconstruction-SOURCE_AUDIT.md}. Source 01 delivered a
checksum manifest, which is not shipped because it describes the delivered
file names; the delivered descriptions of sources 06 and 07 are not shipped
either. Source 13's files are shipped with the sibling report.

\subsection{Where the merge chose}

\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
\item \emph{Base text.} Source 01, which has the weakest hypotheses on most
shared statements (Pell with $D\ne0$, norm forms for any $\Q$-basis and
$c\in\Q^\times$, the only exhaustive quadratic dichotomy, the guard uniform in
$n$, the QE-free homogeneous criterion, the localization discussion).
\item \emph{Decomposable fibers.} Source 02's exact-fiber theorem is printed,
with source 01's rank-$n$ theorem as case (b) of
\cref{odg:thm:decomposable}.
\item \emph{Binary forms.} Source 05's complex form is printed; sources 01 and
02 proved the integer case.
\item \emph{Power equations.} Source 05's separated-power theorem replaces
source 01's gcd$>1$ corollary, which is kept as \cref{odg:rem:powers}.
\item \emph{Isotropic vectors.} Source 02's hypothesis $a\ne0$ replaces
source 01's $q(a)\ne0$ in \cref{odg:lem:nullchoices}.
\item \emph{Pairs without gcd.} Source 02's general theorem is printed; source
01's degree proof is kept as a second route (\cref{odg:rem:nogcd01}) because
it localizes to divisible workspaces.
\item \emph{Homogeneous systems.} Source 01's elementary proof and source 02's
Tarski proof are both kept, since only the latter handles sign conditions
and decidability.
\item \emph{Uniqueness of $\ct$.} Source 01's divisibility proof is printed;
source 05 derives it from the set-sized quotient theorem, which is quoted
and whose proof belongs to the sibling report.
\item \emph{Guards.} Both are printed: source 01's (five auxiliaries, three
squares) and source 05's (six auxiliaries, four squares).
\item \emph{Additions.} Two remarks carry the tag \mergetag:
\cref{odg:rem:separatedidentity} (a direct identity giving the divisibility in
the separated-power proof) and \cref{odg:rem:ringinduction} (existential
induction fails in the pure ring language). Both have complete proofs.
\item \emph{Definability base.} Source 06 is the base of
\cref{odg:def:sec:arithmetic,odg:def:sec:ct,odg:def:sec:reconstruction}: its
existential definitions of $\Pi$ and of the graph of $\ct$ supersede source
07's universal and prenex ones, which are kept as
\cref{odg:def:cor:universal,odg:def:rem:sigma2}, and its homomorphism theorem
covers all unital homomorphisms, where source 07's covers automorphisms.
\item \emph{The order-free system.} The two sources' systems are identical;
source 07's statement (both pairs, intermediate rings) is printed, with source
06 credited (\cref{odg:def:thm:constants}).
\item \emph{Real single polynomials.} The quartics of sources 01 and 05 stay
the printed versions. Source 07's quartic is source 05's up to the
substitution of \cref{odg:def:rem:quarticvariant} and is printed there once;
source 06's quintic, dominated by both, is a remark with the comparison
(\cref{odg:def:rem:quintic}).
\item \emph{Pell divisibility.} Source 07's permutation argument is printed
with source 06's finite-ring argument as a second route
(\cref{odg:def:lem:pelldiv}).
\item \emph{Norms.} Source 07's theorem (any base field, intermediate rings)
and source 13's (\'etale algebras, Gaussian variables) are combined in
\cref{odg:dec:thm:etale}; \cref{odg:thm:norm} of sources 01 and 02 is its
special case.
\item \emph{Fibers.} Source 13's real fiber theorem is
\cref{odg:thm:decomposable}(a), printed once with source 02 as base; its
Gaussian case, converse, kernel remark and rank-deficient example are added.
\item \emph{Transfer and obstructions.} Sources 06, 07 and 13 re-prove the
transfer theorem, printed once (\cref{odg:thm:transfer}); the collapse
obstructions of sources 06 and 07 generalize \cref{odg:prop:notdiophantine} and
are printed as \cref{odg:def:thm:collapse}. Source 07's recursive-saturation
statement is printed, with source 06's countable type as the simpler instance.
\item \emph{Fraction fields.} Source 06's common-denominator lemma is
\cref{odg:thm:fractions}, printed once; the Gaussian consequence is stated in
\cref{odg:def:sec:reconstruction}. Source 06's remark distinguishing $\ct$ from
$\st$ joins \cref{odg:rem:notvaluation}.
\item \emph{Four squares.} Source 06's appendix proof of Lagrange's theorem is
not reprinted; the theorem is cited (\cite{FourSquares} for Mathlib, source
07's \cite{Conrad}).
\item \emph{Further additions.} \Cref{odg:def:cor:ctquartic} (a quartic with six
witnesses for the graph of $\ct$, improving source 06's degree-ten polynomial),
\cref{odg:def:rem:numberfieldideal} (existential definitions over number-field
coefficients), the Gaussian example in \cref{odg:dec:rem:kernels}, the collapse
of the quintic over $\Og$ in \cref{odg:def:rem:quintic}, and the combined
\cref{odg:dec:thm:etale} carry the tag \mergetag{} and have complete proofs.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Printed once.} The floor formula, retraction, congruences, finite
quotients and completions, transfer, Hilbert's tenth problem, the Smith
criterion, units, the Euclid example, non-atomicity, the ACC chain,
$\Pi^2=\Pi$ and non-generation, $\Frac(\Oz)=\No$, the Pythagorean family, the
Fermat scaling, $x^2=\omega^2+1$, the orthogonal group, the nilpotent
isometry, the torus remark, and the failure of four squares for $\omega-1$
appear in two or three of sources 01, 02 and 05 and are printed once, with all
sources named. From sources 06, 07 and 13: the units lemma, Pell rigidity
(with the Gaussian solution $(i,i)$), the Pell divisibility lemma, the
intersective polynomial and its proof, the order-free system and its
Gaussian witness for $1+i$, the transfer theorem and Hilbert's tenth problem,
the collapse obstruction, $\Frac(\Oz)=\No$, the zero Pell fiber, the cubic
norm, the negative-exponent Pell solutions and the omitted types are each
printed once, with every proving source named.
